# Screenshot Coverage Index

One row for every one of the 1,022 frames in `screenshots/`, each caption written from direct visual inspection of that frame.

## Purpose and how to read this ledger

This ledger is the coverage proof for the workflow catalog. It exists to demonstrate that no frame in the
screenshot corpus was silently dropped: every frame has its own row, its own observed caption, and a named flow
and workflow-area document that account for it. Read it as the catalog's traceability index — from any frame you
can reach the flow that uses it and the area document that specifies it, and from any area document's
`Frames covered` set you can come back here and check the arithmetic. The catalog this ledger indexes is listed
in the [Workflow Catalog](README.md).

Every row in the [Ledger](#ledger) carries four columns:

| Column | What it holds |
|---|---|
| `Frame` | A link to the frame in `screenshots/`, written as a percent-encoded relative path. The link text `frame N` always carries the same number as the file it points at. |
| `Observed caption` | One line describing what is actually visible in that specific frame, written from the pixels. |
| `Flow(s)` | The identifier(s) of the named flow(s) that use the frame, in the form `<area>.<flow>` — for example `02.3` is the third flow defined in `02-channels.md`. Every identifier used in the ledger is defined in [Flow groupings](#flow-groupings). |
| `Area document(s)` | The workflow-area document(s) that cover the frame. The **primary owning document is always named first**; every name after it is a secondary cross-reference, and the secondary list is **derived mechanically from the area documents themselves** — an area appears here if and only if that document actually cites this frame. |

Notation used throughout this ledger:

- **Primary owner first, secondaries derived.** Each frame has exactly one primary owning area document — the
  document responsible for specifying it — and that document is always named first. The names after it are
  **not hand-authored**: the secondary list on every row is generated from the area documents' own frame citations,
  so an area is listed as a secondary on a row **if and only if that document cites that frame**. The relation is
  therefore checkable in both directions — from a row to the documents that cite it, and from any document's
  citations back to the rows that list it — and a disagreement is a defect rather than a matter of judgement.
  Secondary cross-references are ignored by the coverage arithmetic, so it is the count of *primary* owners that
  reconciles with the corpus. Across the 1,022 rows there are **691 secondary entries** on **461 rows**, and the areas that
  contribute most of them are [21-states.md](21-states.md), which cites 137 frames it does not own because state is
  a property of other areas' surfaces, and [00-product-overview.md](00-product-overview.md), which cites **330**
  because the shell and the shared component contracts are evidenced from surfaces every other area owns.
- **One flow per frame.** Segmentation produced exactly one owning flow for every frame in this corpus, so the
  `Flow(s)` column carries a single identifier on every row.
- **Flow identifiers** are `<area>.<flow>`. Flow numbers are allocated per area in the order the flows first appear
  in the corpus, so `15.1` is the first flow defined in `15-admin-workspace.md`.
- **Frame spans** in the [Flow groupings](#flow-groupings) table are written as plain numeric ranges, because they
  designate a span rather than cite a single frame. Every individual frame is cited with its full relative link in
  the [Ledger](#ledger).
- **`UNREADABLE`** is the caption of any frame that could not be visually inspected. It would appear alone, with no
  description of any kind, because a description written from anything other than the pixels would be a fabrication.
  **No frame in this corpus is marked `UNREADABLE`:** all 1,022 were inspected.
- **Captions describe product interface only.** The exclusion rule that governs every caption in this ledger, and
  its measurement, are stated once in the [Workflow Catalog](README.md) and nowhere else — including not here.
- **Observed, not recalled.** An unqualified caption asserts what the frame shows. Where a frame is a near-copy of
  its predecessor the caption opens `the same …` and then names the one thing that changed, so the distinction
  between surface and state is never lost.
- **Identical pixels get identical claims.** Where two or more frames are byte-identical, every member's caption
  describes the same visible state: no member asserts a state its own pixels do not show, and no two members make
  claims that exclude one another. Later members additionally record the byte-identity. Anything that differentiates
  such frames — which flow they belong to, for example — comes from the frames around them, so it is stated outside
  the caption and marked `**Inferred:**`. The groups concerned are listed in [Duplicate frames](#duplicate-frames).
- **A cross-cutting area may own very few frames.** `21-states.md` is cross-cutting: it is the primary owner of only
  the two error-page frames and appears as a secondary cross-reference on flows throughout the catalog. Under the
  one-primary-owner rule that is expected, and it is not a coverage gap.

## Coverage assertion

- The corpus contains **exactly 1,022 PNG frames**. Every filename follows the same corpus naming pattern — a fixed
  capture-set prefix, a single space, the frame number, then the `.png` extension — and **zero filenames are
  non-conforming**. That prefix is identical in all 1,022 names and appears percent-encoded in every `Frame` link below.
- The frame indices run **0 through 1021 contiguously, with no gaps**: minimum 0, maximum 1021, 1,022 distinct
  indices, and 1021 − 0 + 1 = 1,022, which is the number of files in `screenshots/`.
- **This ledger contains exactly 1,022 rows — one per frame** — in ascending frame order from 0 to 1021. The row
  count equals the file count.
- Row count alone is not sufficient proof of coverage: one deleted row and one duplicated row cancel out and leave
  the count correct while a frame is missing. The binding assertion is therefore **set equality** — the set of frame
  numbers in this ledger equals `{0 … 1021}` exactly, with no missing frame, no extra frame and no duplicated row.
- **Every row names at least one area document**, and each frame has exactly one primary owner. There are zero
  unassigned rows.
- **Cross-document reconciliation: verified for all twenty-three documents.** The invariant is that the union of
  every workflow-area document's `Frames covered` set equals this ledger's frame set, which equals `{0 … 1021}`. It
  is evaluated per area, and it now holds everywhere:
    - **Delivered and reconciled — all 23 documents.** `00` through `22`, every one of them. Each exists, and each
      one's `Frames covered` set has been compared against this ledger's primary-ownership assignment for it and
      agrees exactly — no missing frame, no extra frame, in either direction, for any area.
    - **The arithmetic closes exactly.** The 23 documents' primary sets partition the corpus: they sum to
      **1,022 frames across 248 flows**, every frame is allocated to exactly one primary owner, and no frame is
      allocated twice or left over. The union invariant is therefore not merely satisfiable but **satisfied**.
  Any disagreement between a document and this ledger is a defect in one of the two, not a difference of
  interpretation, and it is resolved by re-inspecting the frame.
- **How flow counting works here, so the 248 stays exact.** This ledger's `Flow(s)` column names the single
  **primary owning** flow for each frame, and the `Flows` column of the table below counts only flows that own at
  least one frame primarily. An area document may legitimately define further flows whose steps cite frames owned by
  other areas — cross-cutting journeys assembled from secondary citations — and **those flows are deliberately not
  counted here**, because counting them would break the one-flow-per-frame arithmetic. [21-states.md](21-states.md)
  is the clearest case: it owns just one flow primarily, `21.1` over frames 1020 and 1021, and cites **137 further
  frames** as the observed evidence for a state while naming the area that owns each of them — so it contributes
  **1 flow and 2 frames** to the totals below while those 137 secondary citations appear in the `Area document(s)`
  column as secondaries and are counted nowhere in the arithmetic. That is by design, and it is why the totals remain 248 and 1,022 rather than drifting upward as
  cross-cutting flows are added.
- **The table below is the primary-ownership map.** It is the assignment this ledger's rows already make, stated per
  area so an area author knows exactly which frames their document must claim. It is derived from the `Flow(s)` and
  `Area document(s)` columns of the 1,022 rows below, so it is internally consistent with this ledger by
  construction, and it has additionally been checked against all twenty-three documents themselves. The `Frames` column sums to 1,022 and the `Flows` column sums to 248.

| Area document | Flows | Frames owned as primary |

## Flow groupings

248 named flows cover the corpus. A flow is one goal-directed journey; contiguous frames that advance the same
journey are grouped under one flow, and the frames of each flow are listed as the span or spans it occupies.
Every flow identifier that appears in the [Ledger](#ledger) is defined here, and every area document named here is
one of the 23 workflow-area documents in the catalog. The table is ordered by first appearance in the corpus, so it
reads in the same order as the ledger below rather than grouped by area.

Eight flows occupy **more than one span**, for two distinct reasons. In some cases the corpus is a curated export
that re-orders and repeats surfaces, so the same journey recurs at non-adjacent indices. In the others, segmentation
by visual state deliberately overrode numeric adjacency: a frame sitting inside a numerically contiguous run belongs
to a different journey than its neighbours, so it was assigned to the journey its pixels support and the surrounding
flow was left with a gap. Each such flow is listed once, under a single identifier, with each span named:

- `02.1` — Land in the first channel of a new workspace — spans 19, 24, 31–32.
- `05.1` — Open a one-to-one direct message and begin composing — spans 110, 227–229, 256.
- `06.2` — Check huddle connection diagnostics — spans 271–272, 274.
- `06.3` — Use the huddle control bar and set a huddle topic — spans 273, 275–279.
- `14.1` — Set notification preferences and a notification schedule — spans 535, 539–543.
- `14.2` — Review and clear the optional navigation destinations — spans 536, 544.
- `14.3` — Set home and theme preferences — spans 537, 546–548.
- `14.4` — Set audio-and-video preferences — spans 538, 561–563.

The six adjacency overrides that produced the gaps in `06.2`, `06.3`, `14.1`, `14.2`, `14.3` and `14.4`, together
with the three that moved a frame between numerically neighbouring flows without leaving a gap
(`02.4`/`02.5`, `00.3`/`02.11`, `13.2`/`13.3`), are recorded with their visual reasons in the Flow Reconstruction
Methodology of the [Workflow Catalog](README.md).

> **Partial capture:** six flows in this ledger stop short of the terminal action a reader might expect, because the
> corpus does not capture it. Each is named for the journey the frames actually show, and the missing step is stated
> here rather than implied by the flow name.
>
> - `12.1` — Confirm that notification preferences are in effect. The browser's own permission prompt, and the act
>   of granting it, are not captured; the corpus shows only the in-product request banner and this confirmation bar.
> - `10.2` — Edit and save a workflow step. No frame shows the publish control being used or a published workflow;
>   the editor exposes the control and the run ends at a saved step with a confirmation toast.
> - `15.10` — Upgrade the workspace from its trial. No frame shows a completed purchase or an order confirmation;
>   the last captured state is the estimate switched to a yearly period with an entered instrument, and before it a
>   set of three simultaneous field-validation failures.
> - `20.2` — Review and reply to a support request in the administration console. The act of raising a new request is not
>   captured; the first frame already shows an open request alongside a contact-us affordance, and the run ends at an
>   unsent reply.
> - `17.9` — Read a webinar page and its registration form. No frame shows the form submitted or a registration
>   confirmation; every field remains empty.
> - `21.1` — Read an application error page. No frame shows recovery from the error; the pages expose a help-centre
>   link and site navigation, and no subsequent frame returns to a working surface.

## Duplicate frames

Eight groups of frames are **byte-identical** to one another, re-verified by MD5 checksum over the corpus: 8 groups
covering 17 frames, of which 9 are redundant copies. **Every member keeps its own full ledger row**, because the row
count must equal the file count; the later member or members of each group carry a caption that describes the frame
and then records that it is byte-identical to the earlier one.

Three of these groups pair frames at **distant indices** — group 1 (4 and 728, 724 frames apart), group 4
(110, 227 and 256, spanning 146 frames) and group 7 (373 and 545, 172 frames apart). That is direct evidence that
the corpus is a curated export which re-orders and repeats surfaces rather than a linear screen recording, and it
is the standing counter-example to any assumption
that frame N + 1 continues the journey of frame N. Segmentation in this catalog is therefore driven by visual-state
evidence, with numeric adjacency treated as a weak prior only.

| Group | Frames, in index order | Reason for the duplicate rows |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | [frame 4](../../screenshots/Slack%20web%20Jul%202024%204.png), [frame 728](../../screenshots/Slack%20web%20Jul%202024%20728.png) | Byte-identical captures of the same surface, 724 frames apart. The later member is retained with its own row and annotated as a byte-identical duplicate of frame 4. |
| 2 | [frame 19](../../screenshots/Slack%20web%20Jul%202024%2019.png), [frame 31](../../screenshots/Slack%20web%20Jul%202024%2031.png) | Byte-identical captures of the same surface, 12 frames apart. The later member is retained with its own row and annotated as a byte-identical duplicate of frame 19. |
| 3 | [frame 90](../../screenshots/Slack%20web%20Jul%202024%2090.png), [frame 95](../../screenshots/Slack%20web%20Jul%202024%2095.png) | Byte-identical captures of the same surface, 5 frames apart. The later member is retained with its own row and annotated as a byte-identical duplicate of frame 90. |
| 4 | [frame 110](../../screenshots/Slack%20web%20Jul%202024%20110.png), [frame 227](../../screenshots/Slack%20web%20Jul%202024%20227.png), [frame 256](../../screenshots/Slack%20web%20Jul%202024%20256.png) | Byte-identical captures of the same surface spanning 146 frames. Both later members are retained with their own rows and annotated as byte-identical duplicates of frame 110. |
| 5 | [frame 141](../../screenshots/Slack%20web%20Jul%202024%20141.png), [frame 174](../../screenshots/Slack%20web%20Jul%202024%20174.png) | Byte-identical captures of the same surface, 33 frames apart. The later member is retained with its own row and annotated as a byte-identical duplicate of frame 141. |
| 6 | [frame 229](../../screenshots/Slack%20web%20Jul%202024%20229.png), [frame 242](../../screenshots/Slack%20web%20Jul%202024%20242.png) | Byte-identical captures of the same surface, 13 frames apart. Both members carry the identical observed caption; the later member is retained with its own row and annotated as a byte-identical duplicate of frame 229. |
| 7 | [frame 373](../../screenshots/Slack%20web%20Jul%202024%20373.png), [frame 545](../../screenshots/Slack%20web%20Jul%202024%20545.png) | Byte-identical captures of the same surface, 172 frames apart. The later member is retained with its own row and annotated as a byte-identical duplicate of frame 373. |
| 8 | [frame 734](../../screenshots/Slack%20web%20Jul%202024%20734.png), [frame 736](../../screenshots/Slack%20web%20Jul%202024%20736.png) | Byte-identical captures of the same surface, 2 frames apart. The later member is retained with its own row and annotated as a byte-identical duplicate of frame 734. |

One further consequence is worth recording rather than smoothing over: because frames 110, 227 and 256 are the same
image, the direct-message landing surface is claimed by a single flow with three separate spans, while frames 229
and 242 — also identical to each other — are claimed by two different flows. Both rows therefore carry the **same**
observed caption, because the pixels are the same pixels: each shows the sent welcoming message already in the
conversation and an empty composer. **Identical pixels are given identical claims, without exception.**

**Inferred:** the two identical frames occupy different positions in the corpus's journey ordering, which is why
they are segmented into different flows. The basis is the surrounding frames rather than the frames themselves —
[frame 228](../../screenshots/Slack%20web%20Jul%202024%20228.png) shows that same greeting still typed in the
composer, so the capture that follows it reads as the send completing, while
[frame 243](../../screenshots/Slack%20web%20Jul%202024%20243.png) shows the hover action bar revealed on that sent
message with an edited marker at its end, so the capture that precedes it reads as the entry point to acting on a
sent message. Journey position is **not** observable from the identical pixels, and no caption in this ledger
asserts it. Wherever a duplicate frame's flow membership rests on neighbouring evidence in this way, the caption
records only what the shared image shows and the flow assignment carries the inference.

## Non-informative and unreadable frames

**No frame in this corpus is `UNREADABLE`.** All 1,022 frames were visually inspected and every one carries a caption
written from that frame's own pixels, under the exclusion rule stated once in the [Workflow Catalog](README.md).
Nothing was described from anything other than the pixels, and nothing was skipped.

**What the captions do and do not assert, stated precisely rather than as a blanket claim.** A caption asserts only
what is *visible* in its own frame: which elements are present, what they read, how they are laid out, and which
state each one renders. A caption does **not** assert the *cause* of a state, and it must not — a screenshot cannot
show a pointer, a keypress or a network response, so any attribution of a rendered state to hovering, focusing,
clicking or loading is an inference about something outside the pixels. Where such an attribution is worth making
it is made in the owning workflow-area document, once, prefixed `**Inferred:**` with its basis — the hover
treatments on the marketing content card and comparison-table row are the worked example, marked in
[`17-marketing-site.md`](17-marketing-site.md) — and the caption here records the treatment itself instead. Where a
capture shows a change from the frame before it, the caption names the change as a difference between two
renderings rather than as an action that produced it.

Byte-identical frames are the test of that discipline and they meet it: no member of any group in
[Duplicate frames](#duplicate-frames) asserts a state its own pixels do not show, no two members of a group make
mutually exclusive claims, and anything that distinguishes such frames from one another — their flow membership,
for instance — is stated outside the caption and marked as an inference.

No frame is non-informative in the sense of being empty or unusable. Two measured categories are nevertheless
flagged below so that a reader knows which rows carry little new information and why, and so that no one mistakes a
near-copy for a distinct surface or a sparse capture for a broken one.

**Near-duplicate micro-states.** 31 frames differ from their immediate predecessor by a mean absolute pixel
difference below 0.1 of 255, measured on band-cropped thumbnails. Each is a genuine micro-state — a hover, a focus
ring, a checkbox, a single typed field, one row of a menu — not a new surface. All are retained with full rows
because a state change is a documented behaviour; the caption on each row names the one thing that changed.

**Visually sparse captures.** 12 frames have an ink coverage below 2 percent of the analysed viewport, measured as
the fraction of band-cropped pixels differing from the frame's own modal background. These are genuinely near-empty
product surfaces — single-card pages, short forms on flat backgrounds, empty states — not failed captures. Each is
described from what is actually on it.
